<a id="msg_060844d1394aea79016a8e77f1781887d087d9453f92286571"></a>
**Codex** · `msg_060844d1394aea79016a8e77f1781887d087d9453f92286571`

# Repository Architecture & Governance Integrity Audit

## 1. Executive summary

The OS Factory repository is not yet trustworthy as an operating control system.

It contains thoughtful governance language, careful distinctions, explicit authority boundaries, source preservation, and unusually honest statements about unresolved architecture. Those are real strengths.

But the repository currently has what I would call a declarative governance system: the rules are documented, but the mechanisms that make those rules reliably true are mostly absent.

The clearest answer to your question is:

> You are still functioning as the hidden control system at nearly every transition where one state becomes another.

Specifically:

- conversation → durable record
- recommendation → decision
- decision → approved scope
- approved scope → canonical truth
- document → registered object
- candidate → operational standard
- private material → safe-to-use material
- completed work → verified work
- repository instructions → actual runtime behavior
- outside research → architecture decision
- failure → detection and correction

The repository generally records what should happen after you notice something. It does not reliably detect invalid states before they become accepted.

The systematic-debugging review found that the duplicate IDs, mixed folders, stale registry, contaminated plan, unchecked completion checklist, and instruction conflicts are not independent mistakes. They share one root mechanism:

> There is no complete, mechanically testable control chain connecting stated policy to filesystem state and runtime behavior.

The adapted review verdict is:

| Review layer | Verdict |
|---|---|
| Architecture | Not ready for trusted operation |
| Governance | Strong intent, unreliable enforcement |
| Registry | Integrity failure |
| Standards | Defined but inconsistently applied |
| Implementation | Partially implemented manually; no trustworthy baseline |
| Overall | Suitable only for supervised discovery and design |

No files were modified.

---

## 2. Findings ranked by severity

### Critical 1: Repository authority and actual runtime authority disagree

**Observed fact:** The on-disk [AGENTS.md](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/AGENTS.md:17) allows bounded editing throughout the repository after approval. The project instructions actually active in this session grant a narrower documentation exception. The [README](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/README.md:12) tells an operator that the repository `AGENTS.md` governs Codex work.

**Inference:** A person inspecting the repository cannot determine the policy actually controlling the agent.

**Risk:** An action may be allowed by the repository but prohibited by runtime authority, or the reverse. Audit results based only on repository files can therefore be false.

**Root mechanism:** Authority is assembled from repository files, global instructions, platform-injected instructions, skills, plugins, permissions, and session state, but the effective chain is not attested inside the repository.

**Recommended correction:** Establish one explicit authority architecture and identify which source governs each runtime. Reconcile the two AGENTS contracts rather than merely copying language between them.

**Preventive control:** **Invariant.** At session start, produce or validate an effective-authority manifest containing the loaded instruction sources, precedence, versions or hashes, permissions, available tools, and exceptions. Fail closed when a required project contract is missing or mismatched.

OpenAI documents that Codex constructs a layered instruction chain and that more local project guidance overrides earlier guidance. It also loads skills from repository, user, admin, and system locations. Those behaviors make the missing effective-runtime manifest consequential, not theoretical. [OpenAI AGENTS.md documentation](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/agent-configuration/agents-md), [OpenAI skills documentation](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/build-skills).

---

### Critical 2: There is no trustworthy change-history baseline

**Observed fact:** Git reports `No commits yet on main`. Every repository artifact is untracked. There is no remote. The repository itself accurately acknowledges that no baseline exists in [README.md](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/README.md:5) and [current-decisions.md](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/reference/context/current-decisions.md:117).

**Inference:** The current state cannot be compared to an authoritative prior state using Git. The `requesting-code-review` model cannot perform its normal base-versus-head review because neither exists.

**Risk:** Unintended changes, corruption, deletion, or semantic drift may be undetectable. Internal version numbers create an appearance of history without recoverable history.

**Root mechanism:** Git operations were correctly approval-gated, but no temporary replacement was established for integrity, snapshots, or change attribution while the baseline remained deferred.

**Recommended correction:** After exact privacy adjudication, establish the first reviewed baseline. Until then, stop describing completed documentation work as fully verifiable over time.

**Preventive control:** **Invariant.** A document cannot be classified as operationally canonical or migration-complete unless it is part of a recoverable baseline or an equivalent immutable snapshot with a manifest and hashes.

**Unknown:** Filesystem or external backup coverage was not established in this audit.

NIST configuration-management guidance treats configuration monitoring as an ongoing control, not merely a written procedure. [NIST SP 800-128](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/sp/800/128/upd1/final).

---

### Critical 3: “Verified” documentation contains directly observable integrity failures

**Observed facts:**

- Two real documents use `OSF-DIAG-001`: the canonical [Decision-Direction Assessment](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/reference/context/decision-direction-assessment.md:1) and the earlier [experience-design report](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/reference/reports/research/whats-actually-failing--executive-decision-instrument-experience-design--2026-08-25.md:1).
- The provisional [document register](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/reference/reports/research/document-register.yaml:64) registers only the older document and says `pending_registration: []`.
- A conversational sentence was accidentally inserted into the supposedly completed [migration plan](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/work/plans/codex-led/2026-08-25-repository-document-naming-migration.md:83).
- The same plan says all batches were completed, but every item in its execution checklist remains unchecked at [line 142](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/work/plans/codex-led/2026-08-25-repository-document-naming-migration.md:142).
- The canonical diagnostic and naming standard are both marked `provisional_location: true`, despite the workflow saying provisional material should not be promoted to canon while location or authority remains unresolved.

**Inference:** Previous verification checked expected paths and intended changes, but not repository-wide invariants or semantic contamination.

**Risk:** A document can appear approved, registered, canonical, and verified while contradicting the system’s own rules.

**Root mechanism:** The same reasoning system creates, updates, and verifies the artifacts without an independent validator, a state schema, or a clean change set.

**Recommended correction:** Treat the current registry and completion receipts as untrusted until the ID collision, missing registration, contamination, checklist state, and canonical/provisional contradictions are adjudicated together.

**Preventive control:** **Invariant.** Block acceptance when any of these occur:

- duplicate real IDs
- canonical and unresolved-provisional states coexist
- registered paths do not match filesystem metadata
- consequential documents are missing from the register
- completion status conflicts with checklist or receipt state
- uncontrolled text appears between governed sections

---

### High 4: Information architecture mixes fundamentally different jobs

**Observed fact:** `reference/context/` contains current context, decisions, a product design, a governance standard, a source registry, raw sources, and archives. `reference/reports/research/` contains research, supplied sources, candidate standards, candidate architecture, an approved design, and a live registry.

**Inference:** Placement is being used as temporary routing, but temporary placement is already functioning as operational architecture.

**Risk:** Humans and agents must interpret document purpose from prose and metadata every time. Retrieval can return research as authority, context as design, or candidate material as current truth.

**Root mechanism:** The final topology remains explicitly unresolved, but work continued accumulating inside convenient catch-all containers.

**Recommended correction:** Decide boundaries by function and lifecycle, not merely by “reference” versus “work.” Separate at least:

- current context
- decision records
- governance standards
- registries
- product or instrument designs
- reusable assets
- research
- supplied evidence
- archive

**Preventive control:** **Guardrail**, becoming an **invariant** after adoption. A routing schema should reject document types placed outside their allowed boundary unless a time-bounded exception is recorded.

Diátaxis similarly organizes documentation around distinct user needs, while arc42 separates context, constraints, decisions, quality requirements, and risks rather than placing them in one general documentation category. [Diátaxis](https://diataxis.fr/), [arc42 architecture decisions](https://docs.arc42.org/section-9/).

---

### High 5: Decision authority is recorded, but decision provenance is weak

**Observed fact:** [current-decisions.md](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/reference/context/current-decisions.md:1) is a single omnibus summary containing business, website, diagnostic, Factory, documentation, Git, and communication decisions. Individual decisions do not have stable IDs, source-session references, alternatives, rationale, consequences, or supersession links.

**Inference:** Venkat’s memory and conversation history remain necessary to determine whether the summary accurately represents each ruling.

**Risk:** Later systems may preserve the sentence but lose why it was decided, what alternatives were rejected, and the exact scope of approval.

**Root mechanism:** “Decision record” currently means a human-readable digest, not a governed collection of independently identifiable decisions.

**Recommended correction:** Preserve the digest as a current projection, but give consequential decisions individual records or structured entries with stable identity, context, ruling, scope, status, source evidence, consequences, and supersession.

**Preventive control:** **Invariant.** No consequential decision can become canonical without attributable approval evidence and explicit scope. No later document may silently widen that scope.

Arc42 recommends recording important decisions with context, decision, status, consequences, and supersession relationships so stakeholders can retrace them. [arc42 decision guidance](https://docs.arc42.org/section-9/).

---

### High 6: Skills, memory, plugins, and runtime dependencies are outside repository governance

**Observed fact:** The current environment contains 104 memory files, 30 user/system skill definitions, and 36 cached plugin skill definitions outside this repository. The repository contains no local inventory, allowlist, compatibility statement, pinned versions, or conflict-resolution record.

One explicitly used skill normally requires dispatching a subagent. The project contract prohibits delegation without separate approval. I had to interpret and adapt the skill manually.

**Inference:** Runtime behavior depends on mutable external components that the repository cannot identify or reproduce.

**Risk:** A skill update, plugin change, memory change, or platform instruction change can alter behavior without any repository diff.

**Root mechanism:** Agent capabilities are treated as environmental conveniences rather than governed dependencies.

**Recommended correction:** Define an agent-runtime bill of materials: required, allowed, prohibited, and optional instructions, skills, memories, plugins, tools, and versions.

**Preventive control:** **Invariant.** Start only when the effective dependency set satisfies the project profile. At minimum, detect new or changed privileged skills and authority conflicts.

OpenAI confirms skills may be loaded from repository, user, admin, and system scopes, and that same-name skills are not merged. [OpenAI skills documentation](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/build-skills).

---

### High 7: Privacy classification is guidance, while actual access remains broad

**Observed fact:** Raw and archived sources are excluded from Git, but preserved files use ordinary `0644` permissions and directory traversal is not restricted to a private principal. The [README](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/README.md:32) correctly says `.gitignore` does not encrypt or make local access safe.

**Inference:** The current privacy boundary primarily prevents accidental Git tracking. It does not govern local reading, agent retrieval, retention, copying, or downstream use.

**Risk:** Sensitive material can enter model context or be read by another local process even while every Git rule is followed.

**Root mechanism:** Tracking control and information-access control are correctly distinguished in prose, but only tracking has a concrete mechanism.

**Recommended correction:** Define access, retrieval, retention, redaction, and allowed-use rules for `PRIVATE` and `CLIENT-RESTRICTED` material.

**Preventive control:** **Guardrail** for low-risk internal material; **invariant** for client-restricted or credential-bearing material. Use restrictive permissions or an appropriately protected store, plus explicit retrieval authorization and audit logging.

There is no evidence in this audit that data was improperly disclosed. This is an exposure-path finding, not a breach finding.

---

### High 8: Failure detection depends on Venkat noticing something feels wrong

**Observed fact:** There are no active repository hooks, CI checks, schemas, tests, evals, link checks, duplicate-ID checks, registry completeness checks, sensitive-content scans, instruction-drift checks, or approval-evidence checks.

The candidate documentation workflow explicitly says it remains manual at [lines 206–227](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/reference/reports/research/documentation-capture--workflow-standard--2026-08-25.md:206).

**Inference:** The effective observability mechanism is Venkat reviewing outputs and challenging anything that looks wrong.

**Risk:** The system appears controlled until an inconsistency is personally detected. Quiet failures can persist indefinitely.

**Root mechanism:** Human judgment and deterministic validation have not been separated. Because some questions require judgment, almost all validation was left to judgment.

**Recommended correction:** Divide controls into:

- deterministic facts the system can enforce
- evidence checks a reviewer can independently inspect
- consequential judgments Venkat must retain

**Preventive control:** A layered set of **invariants** and **guardrails** around the human decision points.

Both OpenAI and Anthropic now expose hooks that can block operations or enforce validation instead of relying on an LLM to remember. Anthropic explicitly describes hooks as deterministic controls. [OpenAI hooks](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/hooks), [Claude Code hooks guide](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/hooks-guide).

---

### Medium 9: Classification vocabulary has already drifted from the approved standard

**Observed fact:** The naming standard allows types such as `architecture`, `asset`, `audit`, `design`, `research`, and `standard` at [lines 146–165](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/reference/context/document-naming-standard.md:146). Actual metadata uses values including:

- `approved-experience-design`
- `architecture-reconciliation`
- `candidate-factory-primitive`
- `candidate-reasoning-pattern`
- `candidate-workflow-standard`
- `documentation-recovery-audit`
- `provided-source`

Several values combine document type with status or subtype, contrary to the standard’s own separation model.

**Inference:** Metadata was authored for readability without being validated against its controlled vocabulary.

**Risk:** Filtering, routing, and validation cannot rely on `document_type`.

**Root mechanism:** The vocabulary is stated in prose but has no schema or enumerated registry.

**Recommended correction:** Adjudicate the existing values into type, subtype, status, and domain fields.

**Preventive control:** **Invariant.** Validate metadata against a versioned schema and require an explicit standards change before introducing a new controlled value.

---

### Medium 10: References, supersession, and dependencies are mostly human-readable only

**Observed fact:** Many cross-document relationships are plain paths, prose, or IDs inside code formatting rather than machine-resolvable links. There is no dependency graph or reference validator.

**Inference:** Moving or superseding documents safely depends on repository-wide search and human interpretation.

**Risk:** A path can resolve while the semantic dependency is stale. A later artifact may reference a superseded meaning without detection.

**Root mechanism:** Documents act simultaneously as narrative, metadata store, registry, and dependency declaration.

**Recommended correction:** Define explicit relationship fields such as `supersedes`, `derived_from`, `implements_decision`, `governed_by`, and `projects_from`.

**Preventive control:** **Guardrail.** Validate referential integrity and flag references to superseded or noncanonical objects.

---

## 3. Architecture-boundary map

Current effective architecture:

```text
Platform and user environment
├── system and developer instructions
├── injected project contract
├── global memory
├── user and system skills
├── plugin skills and tools
└── permission profile
          │
          │ effective behavior is assembled here
          ▼
Repository
├── AGENTS.md              claimed local authority
├── README.md              operational entrypoint
├── reference/context/     context + decisions + design + standard
│   ├── core/
│   ├── current-decisions.md
│   ├── diagnostic design
│   ├── naming standard
│   ├── source register
│   ├── raw sources
│   └── archive
├── reference/reports/research/
│   ├── research
│   ├── supplied evidence
│   ├── candidate architecture
│   ├── candidate workflow
│   ├── approved design
│   └── live provisional registry
└── work/plans/
          │
          ▼
No committed baseline
No control execution layer
No repository observability layer
```

The missing architectural boundary is between policy and operation:

```text
Written policy
    ↓ missing compilation/validation
Effective runtime policy
    ↓ missing precondition checks
Authorized state transition
    ↓ missing invariant validation
Repository state
    ↓ missing monitoring
Detected failure
```

---

## 4. Canonical-source-of-truth assessment

| Subject | Claimed source | Reliability |
|---|---|---|
| Codex authority | Repository `AGENTS.md` | Low, because runtime contract differs |
| Current business context | `reference/context/core/` | Moderate, but decisions are also duplicated elsewhere |
| Decisions | `current-decisions.md` | Low to moderate, readable but weak provenance |
| Diagnostic design | `decision-direction-assessment.md` | Low until ID collision and predecessor relationship are resolved |
| Naming standard | `document-naming-standard.md` | Moderate as policy, low as enforced truth |
| Document identity | Provisional document register | Low, demonstrably stale |
| Supplied-source inventory | `source-register.md` | Relatively strong for its stated first-pass scope |
| Factory architecture | Candidate reconciliation packet with approved subset | Ambiguous by design; no final canonical architecture |
| Runtime memory and skills | Outside repository | Not governed by repository |
| Historical change state | Git | Absent |

The strongest repository artifact is the [source register](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/reference/context/source-register.md:20): it states scope, distinguishes evidence classes, records exact duplicate findings, and preserves uncertainty. Even there, enforcement and protected access remain external.

---

## 5. Registry and identity integrity assessment

Registry integrity currently fails.

- Stable IDs are not unique.
- The canonical diagnostic is absent from the document register.
- `pending_registration: []` is incorrect.
- The registry is itself Candidate, noncanonical, and provisionally located.
- Registry types differ from document metadata types.
- The registry has no completeness rule defining which objects it must contain.
- The source register and document register have overlapping “registry” roles without a governed boundary.
- The stable-ID example inside the naming standard reuses a live ID, which would create a false duplicate for a naïve parser.

The immediate problem is not choosing a new number for one document. The problem is that no authoritative allocator or uniqueness boundary exists.

---

## 6. Decision and authority assessment

The system correctly preserves Venkat’s authority in prose. It does not yet preserve his rulings with enough structure to make that authority independently inspectable later.

A sound decision transition should be:

```text
Proposal
  → evidence and alternatives
  → explicit ruling
  → bounded approval record
  → owning canonical artifact
  → implemented representation
  → conformance evidence
```

The current repository often jumps from conversation or summary directly to an approved document and then relies on Venkat to remember:

- what exactly was approved
- which wording was merely discussed
- which scope was excluded
- whether later language widened the decision
- whether implementation actually conforms

That is where retained human authority turns into hidden operational dependency.

---

## 7. Standards-to-controls gap map

| Requirement | Current control | Classification | Gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| Venkat retains approval authority | AGENTS and decision prose | Guidance | No approval-evidence validator |
| One canonical owner | Architecture principle | Guidance | Competing representations remain |
| Stable IDs | Naming standard and register | Guidance | Duplicate ID exists |
| Controlled metadata | Written vocabulary | Guidance | Uncontrolled types are in use |
| Preserve private sources | `.gitignore` | Guardrail | No protected access/use boundary |
| No unauthorized Git actions | Runtime permissions and instructions | Guardrail | Effective policy not attested |
| Migration completeness | Narrative receipt | Guidance | Checklist conflicts with completion |
| Register remains current | Candidate workflow | Guidance | Register is stale |
| Verify every edited file | AGENTS instruction | Guidance | No independent or deterministic verification |
| Current decisions beat older material | Context routing rule | Guidance | No supersession or conflict checker |
| Failure detection and safe stopping | Constitutional invariant | Guidance | No operational detector |
| Git provides change history | Planned mechanism | Absent | No baseline |
| Agent runtime uses allowed dependencies | None | Absent | External skills and memory unmanifested |

Very little is currently an invariant inside the repository. Most real guardrails come from the external sandbox and permission profile, not the Factory architecture.

---

## 8. Instruction, skill, hook, and validator assessment

### AGENTS.md

Useful and thoughtfully bounded, but contradicted by the active runtime version. It cannot currently serve as a reliable authority record.

### CLAUDE.md and `.claude/`

Absent. That is not automatically an error during discovery, but it means a future Claude Code implementation session would not receive repository-specific context, permission rules, hooks, or routing through Claude’s standard project surfaces.

Anthropic distinguishes project context in `CLAUDE.md`, permissions in settings, and deterministic controls in hooks. [Claude directory guidance](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/claude-directory), [Claude permissions](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/permissions).

### Skills

Skills are available globally and through plugins, but are not inventoried or reconciled with project authority. The current task demonstrated a real conflict: the requested review skill prescribes a subagent, while the repository prohibits delegation.

### Rules and permissions

The external sandbox provides a useful guardrail. The repository has no local executable rule set. OpenAI rules can control which commands may run outside the sandbox, with the most restrictive match winning. [OpenAI rules](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/agent-configuration/rules).

### Hooks and validators

None are active locally. This is the largest gap between “the AI should remember” and “the system guarantees.”

Priority validator candidates are:

1. effective-authority and dependency manifest
2. duplicate-ID and registry-completeness check
3. metadata schema and controlled-vocabulary check
4. lifecycle-state consistency check
5. approval-source and scope check
6. path, link, and supersession check
7. private-material boundary check
8. contamination and unexpected-diff review
9. baseline and clean-state verification
10. post-change independent integrity review

---

## 9. Where you remain the hidden control system

| Hidden role you are performing | What you personally detect or supply |
|---|---|
| Authority reconciler | Whether the AI was actually allowed to act |
| Research gate | When industry research is required before a design decision |
| Architecture reviewer | Whether a folder or document belongs where it was placed |
| Semantic verifier | Whether a document captures what you meant |
| Approval provenance store | What you approved, rejected, or only discussed |
| Scope controller | Whether an approval was improperly widened |
| Canonical-truth resolver | Which competing document should win |
| ID allocator | Whether an identifier is unique and appropriate |
| Registry auditor | Whether consequential objects are missing |
| Privacy officer | Whether material is safe to track, retrieve, or use |
| Skill compatibility resolver | Whether a skill conflicts with project authority |
| Runtime-drift detector | Whether the active agent rules match the repository |
| Quality evaluator | Whether “verified” actually means trustworthy |
| Observability layer | Noticing quiet failures and asking, “Why does this look wrong?” |
| Incident investigator | Reconstructing how an invalid state was allowed |
| Recovery authority | Deciding what can be repaired, superseded, or removed |

Your consequential judgment should remain. Your role as duplicate detector, metadata parser, permission reconciler, registry checker, and quality alarm should not.

The boundary to aim for is:

> The system prepares a valid, evidence-backed decision state. You make the consequential judgment. The system then proves that the approved judgment was implemented within scope.

---

## 10. Prioritized remediation plan

### Priority 0: Contain false trust

Before further architectural promotion:

1. Label the current registry and completion receipts operationally untrusted.
2. Do not assign more IDs using the current register.
3. Do not describe the repository as having reliable canonical governance.
4. Preserve everything; do not begin opportunistic moves or cleanup.

### Priority 1: Establish the authority and change baseline

1. Reconcile on-disk and runtime AGENTS authority.
2. Define the effective-runtime manifest.
3. Perform the exact privacy review already required.
4. Establish a recoverable baseline after approval.
5. Make future reviews compare an explicit base state to a proposed head state.

### Priority 2: Repair identity and decision integrity

1. Adjudicate the `OSF-DIAG-001` collision.
2. Define the diagnostic documents’ predecessor, supersession, or derivation relationship.
3. Repair registry completeness.
4. Introduce stable decision identities for consequential rulings.
5. Separate current decision projection from canonical decision records.

### Priority 3: Decide the repository’s functional architecture

Adopt boundaries for:

- context
- decisions
- governance
- registries
- product designs
- assets
- research
- source evidence
- archives
- plans and implementation evidence

Then produce a reviewed migration map. Do not move files while deciding the architecture.

### Priority 4: Turn standards into controls

Implement, in order:

1. metadata and ID invariant
2. registry-completeness invariant
3. approval and lifecycle invariant
4. path and dependency guardrail
5. privacy and tracking guardrail
6. completion-receipt consistency check
7. runtime instruction and dependency attestation

### Priority 5: Create Factory-wide review gates

Your proposed adaptation of `requesting-code-review` is sound. The Factory should have independent reviews for:

- architecture readiness
- governance integrity
- registry integrity
- standards conformance
- implementation conformance

Each review should compare:

```text
approved requirement
        vs.
observable implementation
        vs.
validation evidence
        vs.
unresolved risk
```

The review should produce a clear verdict: ready, ready with corrections, or not ready.

### Priority 6: Establish observability and learning

Use NIST’s Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage pattern to keep governance continuous across the lifecycle, including documented risks, measured outcomes, and prioritized responses. [NIST AI RMF Core](https://airc.nist.gov/airmf-resources/airmf/5-sec-core/).

For failures, preserve:

- triggering condition
- invalid state
- why existing controls did not stop it
- detection mechanism
- correction
- preventive control
- control owner
- evidence that recurrence is blocked

That is the Factory-wide adaptation of systematic debugging: do not repair the document until you understand why the system allowed the document to become trusted.

## Recommended next action

The smallest safe next step is not reorganizing folders or fixing the duplicate ID individually.

It is to create one bounded remediation decision packet for the three foundational controls:

1. effective authority source
2. recoverable baseline
3. canonical identity and registry model

Once those three are decided, the remaining corrections can be executed and independently reviewed without requiring you to remain the hidden control system.

---

<a id="msg_060844d1394aea79016a8e7a8039d887d08050af6ab67fbb84"></a>
**Codex** · `msg_060844d1394aea79016a8e7a8039d887d08050af6ab67fbb84`

The attached request is clear and appropriately bounded. Control Backbone v0.1 should be a small, provisional control package, not the beginning of a general governance platform.

I recommend a dependency-free Python CLI with three narrowly separated controls and regression tests. No implementation or Git action has started.

## Current state

### Effective Authority

- The repository contains one root [AGENTS.md](/Users/venkatgullapalli/Documents/my-ai-lab-v2/os-factory/AGENTS.md:1).
- No nested `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/`, repository skills, rules, hooks, or validators exist.
- The active runtime contract differs from the on-disk contract.
- Skills, plugins, memory, tools, and permissions are partly supplied outside the repository.
- A local program cannot inspect all platform-injected instructions. Some authority state will remain explicitly unavailable.

### Trusted Baseline

- Git is initialized on `main`.
- There are no commits.
- All repository files are untracked.
- There is no remote.
- `.gitignore` excludes raw sources, archives, local secrets, private-key formats, and debris.
- Exclusion from Git does not establish safe local access.
- No exact reviewed include set has been approved.

### Stable ID Integrity

- Stable IDs appear primarily in Markdown frontmatter.
- The document register also records IDs, but is provisional and stale.
- Two real documents use `OSF-DIAG-001`.
- The naming standard contains `OSF-DIAG-001` as a code-block example, which must not count as an assignment.
- There is no allocator or uniqueness validator.

## Confirmed requirements

- v0.1 contains exactly three controls.
- It must not depend on final topology, registry architecture, lifecycle design, or full metadata standards.
- Mechanically detectable failures must not rely on model memory.
- Authority observations must distinguish observable, reported, inferred, unavailable, and unknown.
- Baseline creation requires an exact privacy and inclusion review.
- No Git write may occur without explicit approval.
- The existing duplicate ID must remain as a regression case.
- Deferred failures must be recorded without becoming implementation scope.
- Tests must prove the bad cases, not merely the successful cases.

## Derived requirements

- The authority manifest must never call itself complete. It should state its visibility boundary.
- Runtime observations need provenance. Repository-observed facts and agent-reported runtime facts cannot be merged without labels.
- Authority conflicts can only be mechanically detected when both sides have a structured representation.
- The stable-ID scanner must parse actual frontmatter rather than search for ID-shaped strings.
- ID allocation must refuse to proceed while duplicates already exist.
- Baseline readiness must default unadjudicated files to `unclear`, not `safe`.
- Validators need stable exit behavior:

  - `0`: control passes
  - `1`: governed violation detected
  - `2`: control could not make a trustworthy determination

- Generated reports should be reproducible and should not silently become canonical records.

## Design alternatives

### 1. Dependency-free Python CLI, recommended

One provisional package with three commands, JSON policy, JSON output, and standard-library `unittest`.

Advantages:

- Testable parsing and conflict logic.
- No package installation or network dependency.
- Clear modules without creating a platform.
- Portable beyond the current shell.
- Python 3.9.6 is currently available.

Limitation: the current sandbox produces harmless Apple developer-cache warnings when invoking system Python. Test execution will need to verify that these warnings do not affect exit results.

### 2. Shell, Git, `rg`, and `jq`

This would be faster initially but brittle around frontmatter, code blocks, structured errors, fixtures, and cross-platform behavior. It would recreate parsing logic in shell and make false matches more likely.

### 3. General policy engine

A generic schema, registry, approval, lifecycle, and policy engine would offer broader enforcement, but it would directly violate the v0.1 scope guard.

Recommendation: Option 1.

## Proposed design

### Provisional component boundary

Use one self-contained, movable directory such as:

```text
control-backbone/
├── README.md
├── control.py
├── policy/
│   ├── authority.json
│   ├── baseline.json
│   └── id-scope.json
├── known-failures.json
└── tests/
    ├── fixtures/
    ├── test_authority.py
    ├── test_baseline.py
    └── test_ids.py
```

This location is provisional isolation, not approved repository architecture. Nothing outside this component should depend on its physical location.

### Control 1: Effective Authority

Purpose:

Produce the smallest honest answer to “What authority can this session actually observe?”

Mechanism:

```text
Repository-observable sources
          +
Optional runtime-observation input
          ↓
Normalize by source and provenance
          ↓
Compare structured constraints
          ↓
Manifest with conflicts and unknowns
```

The manifest would contain:

- project root
- discovered project instruction files
- file hashes
- declared required instruction files
- repository-visible configuration
- repository-local skills, rules, hooks, and Claude configuration
- externally reported skills, tools, and permissions when supplied
- provenance for every observation
- detected conflicts
- missing required components
- unavailable runtime information
- final status: `pass`, `conflict`, or `indeterminate`

The tool would not attempt to interpret all natural-language instructions automatically. Instead, a reviewed structured authority profile would reference the exact hash of its governing instruction file. If the file changes, the profile becomes stale and the control returns `indeterminate`.

Regression test:

- Fixture project authority: `delegation = deny`
- Fixture runtime observation: `delegation = allow`
- Expected: conflict reported, exit `1`

Important limitation:

The current on-disk/runtime conflict cannot be fully validated by local code unless the active runtime contract is exportable or supplied as a provenance-labeled observation. The manifest must report that limitation.

Deliberately deferred:

- complete platform-instruction attestation
- complete skill and plugin supply-chain governance
- semantic interpretation of arbitrary instructions
- enforcement across other repositories or runtimes

### Control 2: Trusted Baseline

Purpose:

Determine whether an exact Git-visible state is ready for approval and later establish a recoverable comparison point.

Mechanism:

```text
Git-visible candidates
      +
ignore evidence
      +
explicit classification policy
      +
sensitivity indicators
      ↓
Baseline readiness report
```

Classification behavior:

- `safe to include`: only explicitly adjudicated paths
- `must remain excluded`: ignored private sources, archives, secrets, credentials
- `unclear`: default for unreviewed material
- `generated or disposable`: caches, debris, temporary output
- `sensitive`: configured sensitive boundaries or detected secret-like material

The assessment command must be read-only. It cannot stage or commit.

After Venkat approves the exact set, an authorized executor may create the baseline. The baseline identity should be the resulting commit SHA and a clearly bounded commit message. A remote or release tag is unnecessary for v0.1.

Acceptance demonstration:

1. Create the reviewed baseline.
2. Preserve the existing duplicate-ID state as a documented known exception.
3. Apply the separately approved ID correction.
4. Show the exact Git diff from the baseline.
5. Confirm the original baseline remains readable by SHA.
6. Confirm the corrected validator result.

This lets the real ID correction serve as the controlled post-baseline change rather than manufacturing a meaningless test edit.

Deliberately deferred:

- remote backup
- signed commits or tags
- full privacy architecture
- retention policy
- generalized secret management
- release management

### Control 3: Stable ID Integrity

Purpose:

Prevent two real governed objects in the current repository from sharing a stable ID.

Current v0.1 uniqueness boundary:

> Files inside the current repository’s explicitly configured governed scan scope that assign `document_id` in top-level frontmatter.

This does not establish a future ecosystem-wide or federated ID boundary.

Mechanism:

- Parse only top-level frontmatter assignments.
- Ignore code blocks, body examples, quoted discussions, sources, and archives.
- Report ID, assigning path, and assignment location.
- Fail if one ID is assigned to more than one real object.
- Provide `next <prefix>` to scan existing assignments and propose the next unused numeric ID.
- Refuse allocation while any existing duplicate remains.
- Require a successful post-write validation before accepting an assignment.

The allocator will not maintain a persistent ID ledger because that would begin deciding registry architecture.

Known limitation:

The scan-and-suggest allocator is safe for the current single-writer workflow but does not solve concurrent allocation across multiple repositories or agents.

Regression tests:

1. Naming-standard code-block example does not count.
2. Two frontmatter assignments of `OSF-DIAG-001` cause failure.
3. One corrected assignment causes success.
4. `next OSF-DIAG` proposes an unused number.
5. Malformed frontmatter returns `indeterminate`, not a false pass.
6. Excluded sources and archives do not create assignments.

Deliberately deferred:

- global ID namespaces
- federation
- full registry integration
- object types beyond configured frontmatter documents
- lifecycle and supersession validation

## Known Failure Register

| Failure | v0.1 treatment | Future dependency |
|---|---|---|
| Duplicate stable ID | Implement regression control | None |
| Runtime/repository authority conflict | Implement for observable structured inputs | Runtime attestation for complete coverage |
| Incorrect canonical placement | Record only | Repository architecture |
| Content approval interpreted as architecture approval | Record only | Decision and approval model |
| Completion claimed with incomplete evidence | Record only | Completion-evidence model |
| Uncontrolled metadata type | Record only | Metadata schema |
| Stale registry | Record only | Registry scope and ownership |
| Canonical plus unresolved-provisional | Record only | Lifecycle-state model |

## Test and review approach

After design approval, implementation should use test-driven development:

1. Write the authority-conflict failing test.
2. Write the baseline-classification and temporary-Git-repository tests.
3. Write the duplicate-ID tests using the real failure shape.
4. Implement only enough behavior to pass each test.
5. Run all tests and validators.
6. Perform an independent review across:

   - architecture boundaries
   - governance fidelity
   - registry non-expansion
   - standards conformance
   - implementation and test evidence

The review cannot use the current skill’s prescribed subagent because delegation is not authorized. An authorized independent reviewer or a separately approved review session would be required.

## Scope check

The design does not introduce:

- final repository topology
- a canonical registry
- metadata or lifecycle enforcement
- decision-record redesign
- approval validation
- federation
- generalized governance
- completion validation
- complete privacy architecture

The `control-backbone/` directory is deliberately self-contained and provisional. The policy files describe only what these three controls need.

## Approval boundary

Four boundaries remain:

1. **Design approval:** Approve or revise this design before any implementation planning.
2. **Implementation authority:** The current Codex project contract does not authorize Codex to write or run implementation code. After design approval, I can prepare the implementation plan and an exact handoff for an authorized implementation system.
3. **Baseline approval:** After implementation produces the read-only readiness assessment, you must approve the exact Git-visible file set, commit action, and commit message.
4. **ID correction:** You must approve which document retains `OSF-DIAG-001` and the replacement identity for the other document.

There is also a direct skill conflict: the brainstorming skill normally requires committing a design document and dispatching a subagent review. Git writes and delegation are not currently authorized, so I have surfaced that conflict and stopped at the design gate.

Recommended decision: approve this design, including the current-repository uniqueness boundary and the dependency-free Python approach. After that, I’ll produce the bounded implementation plan and authorized-executor handoff.

# Control Backbone v0.1 Implementation Plan

**Goal:** Implement the three approved controls inside `control-backbone/` using Python 3.9 standard-library code and TDD.

**Architecture:** One self-contained provisional component with a thin CLI, three independent control modules, JSON policy, deterministic exit codes, fixtures, and `unittest` regression tests.

**Prohibited:** Git writes, external file changes, delegation, subagents, dependency installation, or deferred v0.2 controls.

## File map

```text
control-backbone/
├── README.md
├── .gitignore
├── control.py
├── backbone/
│   ├── __init__.py
│   ├── result.py
│   ├── authority.py
│   ├── baseline.py
│   └── stable_ids.py
├── policy/
│   ├── authority.json
│   ├── baseline.json
│   └── id-scope.json
├── known-failures.json
├── evidence/
│   └── runtime/                 generated, ignored locally
└── tests/
    ├── test_authority.py
    ├── test_baseline.py
    ├── test_stable_ids.py
    ├── test_cli.py
    └── fixtures/
        ├── authority/
        ├── baseline/
        └── stable-ids/
```

`result.py` will standardize:

- status
- exit code
- observations
- conflicts or violations
- unknowns
- visibility limitations
- evidence provenance

## Task 1: Build the testable CLI boundary

1. Invoke `test-driven-development`.
2. Create the component skeleton.
3. Write failing CLI tests for:

   - valid command routing
   - JSON output
   - exit codes `0`, `1`, and `2`
   - invalid arguments

4. Run the tests and confirm they fail for missing behavior.
5. Implement only the shared result and command-routing behavior.
6. Run the tests and confirm they pass.

Command:

```bash
python3 -m unittest control-backbone/tests/test_cli.py -v
```

## Task 2: Effective Authority control

Write failing tests for:

- root and nested `AGENTS.md` discovery
- instruction-file hashing
- required instruction missing
- stale structured profile hash
- optional runtime observations
- repository/runtime conflict
- unavailable platform state reported as unavailable
- unknown invisible runtime state not presented as observed

Regression expectation:

```text
repository delegation = deny
runtime observation delegation = allow
→ exit 1
→ conflict shown in JSON
```

Important behavior:

- Expected invisible platform state may appear under `visibility_limits` while the control still returns `0`.
- Missing or stale required observable inputs return `2`.
- Detectable conflicts return `1`.
- The control never claims to represent the complete effective platform policy.

Mandatory README trigger:

```text
Run at the beginning of every working session.
Governed work must not silently continue after exit 1 or 2.
```

Proposed command:

```bash
python3 control-backbone/control.py authority \
  --repo . \
  --output control-backbone/evidence/runtime/effective-authority.json
```

## Task 3: Stable ID Integrity control

Write failing tests for:

- real `document_id` assignments in top-level Markdown frontmatter
- code-block examples ignored
- body-text examples ignored
- excluded source and archive boundaries ignored
- malformed frontmatter returning `2`
- duplicate real assignments returning `1`
- unique assignments returning `0`
- next-number calculation
- allocation refusal while duplicates exist

Regression fixtures will demonstrate:

```text
duplicate fixture → exit 1
corrected fixture → exit 0
```

The real repository will remain unchanged and should produce:

```text
OSF-DIAG-001 duplicate → exit 1
```

Mandatory README trigger:

```text
Run before assigning any new document_id.
Do not assign the proposed ID unless discovery and validation succeed.
```

Commands:

```bash
python3 control-backbone/control.py ids check --repo .
python3 control-backbone/control.py ids next --repo . --prefix OSF-DIAG
```

Validator evidence must be attached to the later ID-correction batch.

## Task 4: Trusted Baseline control

Write failing tests using temporary fixture repositories for:

- no Git repository
- repository with no commits
- ignored files
- explicitly safe files
- explicitly excluded files
- generated or disposable files
- sensitive filename or content indicators
- unclassified files
- Git-visible sensitive files
- complete reviewed classification
- baseline SHA existence and recoverability
- controlled change visible against a fixture baseline

Exit behavior:

- `0`: every Git-visible candidate is explicitly classified safe and no violation exists
- `1`: sensitive or must-exclude material is Git-visible for inclusion
- `2`: unclassified material, missing Git state, or inability to determine readiness

Mandatory README trigger:

```text
Run before any staging, commit, tag, or other Git write.
The assessment does not authorize Git action.
```

Command:

```bash
python3 control-backbone/control.py baseline assess \
  --repo . \
  --output control-backbone/evidence/runtime/baseline-readiness.json
```

The real report must explicitly include:

```json
{
  "known_defect_in_proposed_baseline": {
    "id": "OSF-DIAG-001",
    "condition": "duplicate stable ID",
    "baseline_meaning": "reviewed starting state, not evidence of clean ID integrity"
  }
}
```

## Task 5: Record deferred regression cases

Create `known-failures.json` with exactly the approved failure set.

Only these two receive v0.1 controls:

- duplicate stable ID
- detectable runtime/repository authority conflict

Everything else remains `record_only` with:

- why it matters
- expected future behavior
- blocking dependency
- likely future control
- target phase when known

A test will fail if a `record_only` entry is accidentally marked implemented.

## Task 6: Document mandatory operation

The README will state:

| Control | Mandatory trigger | Blocking outcome | Required evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Authority | Session start | Exit 1 or 2 | Authority manifest |
| Baseline | Before any Git write | Exit 1 or 2 | Baseline approval packet |
| Stable ID | Before assigning `document_id` | Exit 1 or 2 | Assignment or correction packet |

It will also state:

- controls do not grant authority
- passing does not prove invisible platform state
- baseline assessment does not authorize Git
- ID validation is repository-local
- outputs must accompany the relevant approval packet
- v0.2 controls remain deferred

## Task 7: Full verification

Run:

```bash
python3 -m unittest discover -s control-backbone/tests -v
```

Then run each validator against its fixtures and inspect the JSON:

```bash
python3 control-backbone/control.py authority \
  --repo control-backbone/tests/fixtures/authority/conflict

python3 control-backbone/control.py baseline assess \
  --repo control-backbone/tests/fixtures/baseline/ready

python3 control-backbone/control.py ids check \
  --repo control-backbone/tests/fixtures/stable-ids/duplicate

python3 control-backbone/control.py ids check \
  --repo control-backbone/tests/fixtures/stable-ids/corrected
```

Expected exit sequence:

```text
authority conflict: 1
baseline ready fixture: 0
duplicate IDs: 1
corrected IDs: 0
```

Then run against the real repository:

- authority manifest: report actual observable limits
- ID validator: expected `1` before correction
- baseline readiness: report actual classifications and known duplicate exception

## Task 8: Scope and implementation review

Perform the adapted five-part review:

- architecture: self-contained and provisional
- governance: no authority creation or widening
- registry: no registry architecture introduced
- standards: deterministic outputs and documented triggers
- implementation: tests exercise real behavior and exit codes

Explicitly search for prohibited scope:

- lifecycle model
- approval validator
- registry completeness
- canonical placement
- federation
- generalized governance
- full dependency governance
- generalized completion validation

Any such implementation is removed or reported as scope violation.

## Gate after implementation

I will stop after presenting:

1. test results
2. fixture validator results
3. real authority manifest
4. real duplicate-ID failure evidence
5. baseline readiness assessment
6. exact proposed baseline include/exclude/unclear classifications
7. explicit known-defect exception
8. all remaining human and model-memory dependencies

No Git action or ID correction will occur.

Afterward, two separate approvals remain:

- Boundary 3: exact baseline commit
- Boundary 4: which document retains `OSF-DIAG-001` and the replacement ID

Please approve this implementation plan, and I’ll begin TDD implementation inside `control-backbone/` only.

Yes, but only in a few useful ways. The core implementation plan remains valid.

What I verified:

- Both `mv` commands failed because the migrations had already been completed. The intended destinations exist, so those commands caused no change.
- The archive checksum passes.
- `reference/`, `work/plans/`, `AGENTS.md`, `README.md`, and `.gitignore` are read-only.

## Plan adjustments

I’ll add these implementation constraints:

1. All code, fixtures, generated evidence, caches, and test-temporary files must remain under `control-backbone/`.
2. Tests will not use the system `/tmp`, because this environment has shown temporary-directory restrictions. They’ll use `control-backbone/tests/.tmp/`.
3. `control-backbone/.gitignore` will locally exclude runtime evidence, test-temporary files, and Python caches. The protected root `.gitignore` will not be touched.
4. Implementation preflight will verify that `control-backbone/` is writable and protected areas are not.
5. The baseline readiness report will record the tar archive as a verified pre-v0.1 recovery snapshot, but not as the Trusted Baseline. It does not provide Git comparison history.
6. The later ID-correction gate must include temporary write authorization for the exact affected files, because `reference/` is now protected.

## One important security issue

The archive contains `.git`, raw sources, and the complete archived context bundle. It is currently readable by group and other local users:

```text
-rw-r--r-- ../os-factory-pre-v01-20260826.tar.gz
```

The checksum proves integrity, not confidentiality. I recommend that you run:

```bash
chmod 600 ../os-factory-pre-v01-20260826.tar.gz ../os-factory-pre-v01.sha256
```

That action is outside my authorized write boundary, so I did not perform it.

Also, the read-only permissions are a strong guardrail against editing content inside those directories, but they are not a complete invariant: because the repository root remains writable, an authorized local process could potentially rename or remove an entire protected top-level directory. Our scoped authority remains an additional necessary control.

Subject to the archive-permission correction, the revised implementation plan is ready for your approval.

It does not comprehensively determine whether the 48 files contain personal information, client-confidential material, sensitive provenance, restricted quotations, or other content that should not enter permanent Git history.

A local commit is not publication, but it creates a persistent historical copy. Removing a sensitive file later does not remove it from Git history.

Therefore:

- No commit should happen yet.
- Nothing is currently staged or committed.
- The 48-file list is only the proposed privacy-review population.
- The next step should be a read-only, file-by-file privacy and security readiness review.
- That review should produce an exact `include`, `exclude`, or `needs-decision` classification and then stop for your approval.

The earlier governing instruction explicitly required privacy/security controls to be established and verified, followed by a stop before staging or committing. I should have preserved that gate instead of asking for commit authorization.
